The product of the two Vandermonde products is a nonzero integer of absolute value at most $M^{k(k-1)}$. Fewer than $k^2(k-1)+1$ primes of size at least $M^{1/k}$ can divide it. Hence some prime $r$ in our list makes these first $k$ coordinates distinct modulo $r$ on each side.

For such a prime put $$F_r(\boldsymbol\alpha)=
 \sum_{\substack{1\le z_1,\ldots,z_k\le M\\
                  z_i\not\equiv z_j\pmod r\ (i\ne j)}}
 \mathrm{e}\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^k\alpha_j\sum_{i=1}^kz_i^j\right).$$ The relevant number of solutions is bounded by $$(s+k)^{2k}\sum_r\int_{[0,1]^k}|F_r|^2|f|^{2s}.$$ The integrands are nonnegative. Write $f=\sum_{a\bmod r}f_a$, where $f_a$ is restricted to $n\equiv a\pmod r$. Hölder’s inequality gives $$|f|^{2s}\le r^{2s-1}\sum_{a\bmod r}|f_a|^{2s}.$$

Fix $a$. In the system counted by $\int|F_r|^2|f_a|^{2s}$, translate all variables by $-a$. Translation preserves the equations for the first $k$ powers by the binomial formula. The remaining $s$ variables on either side are multiples of $r$, so the first lists $\boldsymbol z,\boldsymbol z'$ obey <a id="lp:power-congruences"></a>$$\label{lp:power-congruences}
 \sum_{i=1}^kz_i^j\equiv\sum_{i=1}^k(z_i')^j\pmod{r^j}
 \qquad(1\le j\le k).$$ There are at most $M^k$ choices for the first list. For each such list, there are at most $k!r^{K-k}$ choices for the second. To see this, lift the prescribed $j$th sum modulo $r^j$ to a residue modulo $r^k$. The number of choices for all lifts is $\prod_{j=1}^k r^{k-j}=r^{K-k}$. For each full vector of sums modulo $r^k$, Newton’s identities determine the multiset of roots modulo $r$, since $r>k$. There are at most $k!$ orderings. The Jacobian of the power sums has determinant $$k!\prod_{i<j}(z_j'-z_i'),$$ up to sign, and is invertible modulo $r$. Each ordering therefore lifts uniquely from modulus $r$ to modulus $r^k$: at each stage the next digits are the unique solution of the corresponding linear system modulo $r$. Finally, an interval of $M$ integers contains at most one representative of any residue modulo $r^k$, because $M\le r^k$.

After both first lists are fixed, divide the remaining variables by $r$. They range over a common interval of at most $\lceil M/r\rceil$ integers and have prescribed differences of power sums. Translation to an initial interval changes only these prescribed differences. The count is a Fourier coefficient of the nonnegative function $|f|^{2s}$ at that shorter length, and consequently is at most $J_{s,k}(\lceil M/r\rceil)$. Summing over $a$ accounts for the final factor $r$, and gives <a id="lp:recurrence-count"></a>$$\label{lp:recurrence-count}
 \begin{split}
 J_{s+k,k}(M)
 &\le 2k^{s+k}M^{k-1}J_{s+k,k}(M)^{1/2}\\
 &\quad +(s+k)^{2k}(2k^3+5)k!
       \max_r r^{2s+K-k}M^kJ_{s,k}(\lceil M/r\rceil).
 \end{split}$$

Insert <a href="#lp:inductive-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:inductive-bound">[lp:inductive-bound]</a>. Since $E_s\ge0$, rounding costs at most $2^{E_s}$, and the exponent of $r$ becomes $$2s+K-k-E_s=k^2-\epsilon_s\ge0.$$ Replacing $r$ by at most $2M^{1/k}$ therefore gives the exponent $$k+E_s+\frac{k^2-\epsilon_s}{k}
 =2(s+k)-K+(1-1/k)\epsilon_s.$$ The inequality $J\le a\sqrt J+b$ implies $J\le2a^2+2b$. The exponent $2k-2$ from $a^2$ is admissible: the target exponent starts at $2k$, and at each step its increase is $2k-\epsilon_s/k\ge2k-K/k>0$. Thus the asserted iteration holds. Its constants can be chosen with $$\log C_{s+k}\le \log(1+C_s)
 +O\!\left((s+k)\log(2k)+k\log(s+k)+k^3\log(2k)\right),$$ including <a href="#lp:small-M-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:small-M-cost">[lp:small-M-cost]</a>.

After $O(k\log(2k))$ steps, $\epsilon_s=K(1-1/k)^{(s-k)/k}\le1/100$. Then $s=O(k^2\log(2k))\le C_1k^4$, and summing the displayed costs gives $\log C_s\le C_1k^{C_2}$ for absolute constants. Increasing the exponent from $\epsilon_s$ to $1/100$ proves the Lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Logarithmic phases on progressions

The quantitative moment bound now gives cancellation for a logarithmic phase even when the Taylor degree grows with $x$. This step yields the second estimate from Section <a href="#sec:inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inputs">2</a> and will also control Dirichlet series near the line of absolute convergence.

<a id="stmt-lp-log-phase"></a>
<div id="lp:log-phase" class="lemma">

<a id="lp:progression-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 27** (A logarithmic phase on progressions). *Fix $C>0$. There is an absolute constant $C_3>0$ such that, for sufficiently large $x$ in terms of $C$, the following holds uniformly: $$\exp(L/T^2)\le N'\le2x^5,\qquad q\le L^C,\qquad
 \exp(L/(2T^2))\le |v|\le4x^3.$$ For every residue $a\pmod q$ and every interval $I\subset[N',2N']$, $$\label{lp:progression-estimate}
 \left|\sum_{\substack{n\in I\\n\equiv a\pmod q}}n^{iv}\right|
 \ll \frac{N'}q\exp(-L/T^{C_3}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lp-log-phase"><summary>Proof of stmt-lp-log-phase</summary>

*Proof.* Put $H=N'/q$ and $y=\log|v|/\log H$. Then <a id="lp:phase-scales"></a>$$\label{lp:phase-scales}
 \log H\ge L/T^2-CT\gg L/T^2,\qquad y\ll T^2.$$ Writing $n=q(b+a/q)$, with $0\le a<q$, reduces the sum, up to a factor of modulus one, to $\sum_{b\in\mathcal I}(b+a/q)^{iv}$, where $\mathcal I$ is an interval of integers and $H\le b+a/q\le2H$.

If $y<4/5$, the derivative of $v\log(b+a/q)/(2\pi)$ is monotone, has magnitude comparable to $|v|/H$, and has magnitude less than $1/2$. The monotone first derivative estimate in Lemma <a href="#lp:pre-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:pre-derivatives">25</a> therefore bounds the sum by $O(H/|v|)$. The lower bound on $|v|$ makes this smaller than <a href="#lp:progression-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:progression-estimate">[lp:progression-estimate]</a>.

<a id="lp:shifted-phase"></a>
Suppose now that $y\ge4/5$. Set $$M=\lfloor H^{3/4}\rfloor,\qquad k=\lceil4y+8\rceil.$$ Averaging the sum over forward shifts $1\le h\le M$ changes it by $O(M)$: a shift changes an interval at only $O(h)$ endpoints. For $b\in\mathcal I$, Taylor expansion gives $$\frac{v}{2\pi}\log(b+h+a/q)
 =\frac{v}{2\pi}\log(b+a/q)
       +\sum_{j=1}^k\alpha_j(b)h^j+O(H^{-2}),
 \qquad
 \alpha_j(b)=\frac{(-1)^{j-1}v}{2\pi j(b+a/q)^j}.$$ Indeed the remainder is $O(|v|(M/H)^{k+1})=O(H^{y-(k+1)/4})=O(H^{-9/4})$, uniformly even as $k$ grows. Thus, with $$S(\boldsymbol\alpha)=\sum_{h=1}^M
                 \mathrm{e}\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^k\alpha_jh^j\right),$$ the original sum is bounded by $$\label{lp:shifted-phase}
 \frac1M\sum_{b\in\mathcal I}|S(\boldsymbol\alpha(b))|
       +O(M+H^{-1}).$$

<a id="lp:occupancy"></a>
Partition the coefficient torus $[0,1)^k$ into boxes with side length $M^{-j}$ in coordinate $j$. Each box contains at most $$\label{lp:occupancy}
 \exp(O(k))H^{4/5}$$ of the vectors $\boldsymbol\alpha(b)\pmod1$. For this it suffices to use the coordinate $j=\lceil y+3/20\rceil$, which lies between $1$ and $k$. Before reduction modulo one, this coordinate has magnitude $O(H^{-3/20})$, is monotone, and has derivative of magnitude at least $$\frac{|v|}{2\pi(2H)^{j+1}}
       \ge\exp(-O(k))H^{y-j-1}.$$ A coordinate interval of length $M^{-j}$ on the torus pulls back to at most two intervals in $b$. Their total length is at most $\exp(O(k))H^{1+j/4-y}$. Here the floor in $M$ costs $\exp(O(k))$, while $$y-\frac j4\ge\frac{3y}4-\frac{23}{80}
       \ge\frac5{16}>\frac15.$$ Counting integer points proves <a href="#lp:occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:occupancy">[lp:occupancy]</a>.

<a id="lp:box-supremum"></a>
Let $s$ be supplied by Lemma <a href="#lp:mean-value" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:mean-value">26</a>. We also need the following bound for suprema over boxes $Q$: $$\label{lp:box-supremum}
 \sum_Q\sup_{\boldsymbol\alpha\in Q}|S(\boldsymbol\alpha)|^{2s}
 \le\exp(O(sk+k))M^KJ_{s,k}(M).$$ To prove it, rescale each box to $[0,1]^k$. Iterating the one-dimensional fundamental theorem of calculus gives, for any smooth function $F$ on that cube, $$\sup|F|\le\sum_{\boldsymbol\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^k}
       \int_{[0,1]^k}|\partial^{\boldsymbol\epsilon}F|.$$ Hölder’s inequality bounds the $2s$th power of the right-hand side by $2^{k(2s-1)}$ times the sum of the corresponding $2s$th moments. For the rescaled $S$, every mixed derivative has coefficients bounded in modulus by $(2\pi)^k$: each differentiation in coordinate $j$ introduces the factor $2\pi i h^j/M^j$. On summing over the boxes, change of variables contributes $M^K$. Orthogonality then bounds the full-torus moment of every such derivative by $(2\pi)^{2sk}J_{s,k}(M)$. This proves <a href="#lp:box-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:box-supremum">[lp:box-supremum]</a>, with constants controlled at the growing degree.

<a id="lp:phase-final-bound"></a>
Apply Hölder’s inequality to the sum in <a href="#lp:shifted-phase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:shifted-phase">[lp:shifted-phase]</a>, and use <a href="#lp:occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:occupancy">[lp:occupancy]</a>, <a href="#lp:box-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:box-supremum">[lp:box-supremum]</a>, and <a href="#lp:mean-value-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:mean-value-bound">[lp:mean-value-bound]</a>. Since $|\mathcal I|\ll H$, the result is $$\label{lp:phase-final-bound}
 \frac1M\sum_{b\in\mathcal I}|S(\boldsymbol\alpha(b))|
 \ll H\left(\exp(O(k^{C_4}))H^{-1/5}M^{1/100}\right)^{1/(2s)}$$ for an absolute constant $C_4$. We have $k\ll T^2$ and $s\ll T^8$, whereas $$-\frac15\log H+\frac1{100}\log M
       \le-\frac{77}{400}\log H.$$ Every fixed power of $T$ is $o(L/T^2)$. Thus the right-hand side of <a href="#lp:phase-final-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:phase-final-bound">[lp:phase-final-bound]</a> is at most $H\exp(-c_1L/T^{10})$ for some absolute $c_1>0$, once $x$ is sufficiently large. The errors in <a href="#lp:shifted-phase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:shifted-phase">[lp:shifted-phase]</a> are smaller. Increasing a fixed exponent $C_3>10$ absorbs $c_1$ and proves the Lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### A zero-free strip from the phase estimate

We next convert progression cancellation into a bound for a Dirichlet $L$-function near $\operatorname{Re}s=1$. A local logarithmic-derivative formula and the classical positive trigonometric polynomial then exclude zeros in the narrower strip needed for Mellin inversion.

<a id="lp:L-tail"></a>
We write $D(s,\chi)$ for the Dirichlet $L$-function, to distinguish it from $L=\log x$. Characters need not be primitive. Periodicity gives $\sum_{n\le u}\chi(n)=c_\chi u+O(q)$, where $c_\chi=q^{-1}\sum_{a\bmod q}\chi(a)$. Partial summation therefore continues $D(s,\chi)$ meromorphically to $\operatorname{Re}s>0$, with only the possible simple pole at $s=1$, and gives, for $\sigma=\operatorname{Re}s$ in a fixed compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$, $$\label{lp:L-tail}
 D(s,\chi)=\sum_{n\le Y}\frac{\chi(n)}{n^s}
       +\frac{c_\chi Y^{1-s}}{s-1}
       +O\bigl(q(1+|s|)Y^{-\sigma}\bigr).$$

<a id="stmt-lp-l-upper"></a>
<div id="lp:L-upper" class="lemma">

<a id="lp:L-upper-bound"></a>
**Lemma 28** (A bound near the line $\operatorname{Re}s=1$). *Fix $C>0$ and put $r_*=T^4/L$. Uniformly for $q\le L^C$, $$\label{lp:L-upper-bound}
 \log|D(\sigma+iv,\chi)|\ll_C T^2
 \quad\left(|\sigma-1|\le10r_*,\quad
                   \frac12\le|v|\le3x^3\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lp-l-upper"><summary>Proof of stmt-lp-l-upper</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose $|v|\le\exp(L/(2T^2))$, and use $Y=\exp(2L/T^2)$ in <a href="#lp:L-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:L-tail">[lp:L-tail]</a>. Absolute summation gives $$\sum_{n\le Y}n^{-\sigma}
 \ll(1+\log Y)\max(1,Y^{1-\sigma})\le\exp(O(T^2)).$$ Since $|s-1|\ge1/2$, the pole term has the same bound. The error is at most $$\exp\left(-\frac{3L}{2T^2}+O(T^2+CT)\right),$$ and hence is negligible.

For $\exp(L/(2T^2))<|v|\le3x^3$, take $Y=x^5$. The terms with $n\le\exp(L/T^2)$ again contribute $\exp(O(T^2))$ in absolute value. On a dyadic interval above this threshold, sum <a href="#lp:progression-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:progression-estimate">[lp:progression-estimate]</a>, with phase $-v$, over the residue classes modulo $q$, including their character coefficients. The factors $q$ and $1/q$ cancel. Partial summation with $n^{-\sigma}$ bounds that dyad by $$\exp(-L/T^{C_3})\exp(O(T^4)).$$ There are $O(L)$ dyads, and the same bound applies to a final partial dyad. Their total is negligible, since $L/T^{C_3}$ exceeds every fixed power of $T$. Finally, the pole term and remainder in <a href="#lp:L-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:L-tail">[lp:L-tail]</a> are bounded respectively by $$\exp\left(-\frac{L}{2T^2}+O(T^4)\right),\qquad
 \exp\bigl(-2L+O(T^4+CT)\bigr).$$ This proves <a href="#lp:L-upper-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:L-upper-bound">[lp:L-upper-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lp-local-log-derivative"></a>
<div id="lp:local-log-derivative" class="lemma">

<a id="lp:local-log-derivative-formula"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Local logarithmic derivative). *Fix $C>0$, let $q\le L^C$, and put $s_0=1+r_*+iv$, where $1\le|v|\le\tfrac52x^3$. There is a radius $R$ with $4r_*\le R\le5r_*$, with no zero on its boundary, for which $$\label{lp:local-log-derivative-formula}
 \frac{D'}{D}(s,\chi)
 =\sum_{|\rho-s_0|<R}\frac1{s-\rho}
       +O_C(T^2/r_*)\qquad(|s-s_0|\le2r_*),$$ away from zeros. The sum counts zeros with multiplicity and has $O_C(T^2)$ terms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lp-local-log-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-lp-local-log-derivative</summary>

*Proof.* For large $x$, the disk $|s-s_0|\le8r_*$ avoids the possible pole at $s=1$ and lies in the region of Lemma <a href="#lp:L-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:L-upper">28</a>. At its center the Euler product gives $$|D(s_0,\chi)|\ge\prod_p(1+p^{-1-r_*})^{-1}
       =\frac{\zeta(2+2r_*)}{\zeta(1+r_*)}\gg r_*.$$ Thus $\log|D(s_0,\chi)|\ge-O(T)$. Jensen’s formula, using the radius $8r_*$ and the upper bound $O_C(T^2)$, shows that the disk of radius $5r_*$ contains $O_C(T^2)$ zeros. Choose $R\in[4r_*,5r_*]$ so that none lies on its boundary.

Use centered coordinates $z=s-s_0$, and write $a=\rho-s_0$ for each zero in $|z|<R$. Divide $D(s_0+z,\chi)$ by the disk Blaschke factors $$B_a(z)=\frac{R(z-a)}{R^2-\overline a z},$$ repeated with multiplicity. The quotient $G$ is holomorphic and nonvanishing on the closed disk, and has the same boundary modulus as $D$. Maximum modulus gives $\log|G|\le M_0$ throughout the disk for some $M_0=O_C(T^2)$. Since $|B_a(0)|<1$, $\log|G(0)|\ge-O(T)$.

Let $h$ be an analytic logarithm of $G$. The positive harmonic function $M_0-\operatorname{Re}h$ has value $O_C(T^2)$ at the center. The Poisson formula, or its derivative together with Harnack’s inequality on concentric disks, gives $$|h'(z)|\ll_C T^2/r_*\qquad(|z|\le2r_*).$$ Restoring the factors uses $$\frac{B_a'}{B_a}(z)=\frac1{z-a}
       +\frac{\overline a}{R^2-\overline a z}.$$ The second term is $O(1/r_*)$ on $|z|\le2r_*$, uniformly in $|a|<R$. There are $O_C(T^2)$ such terms, giving exactly <a href="#lp:local-log-derivative-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:local-log-derivative-formula">[lp:local-log-derivative-formula]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lp-zero-free"></a>
<div id="lp:zero-free" class="lemma">

<a id="lp:zero-free-region"></a>
<a id="lp:log-derivative-bound"></a>
**Lemma 30** (A zero-free strip at large height). *For each fixed $C>0$ there is $c=c(C)>0$ such that every character of modulus at most $L^C$ has no zero in $$\label{lp:zero-free-region}
 \operatorname{Re}s\ge1-cT^2/L,\qquad
                    1\le|\operatorname{Im}s|\le x^3.$$ Moreover, $$\label{lp:log-derivative-bound}
 \left|\frac{D'}D(s,\chi)\right|\ll_C L
 \quad\left(1-\frac{cT^2}{2L}\le\operatorname{Re}s\le1+\frac1L,
       \quad2\le|\operatorname{Im}s|\le\frac{x^3}{2}\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lp-zero-free"><summary>Proof of stmt-lp-zero-free</summary>

<a id="lp:trigonometric-positivity"></a>
*Proof.* For $\sigma>1$, the Euler products and the inequality $3+4\cos\theta+\cos(2\theta)=2(1+\cos\theta)^2\ge0$ give $$\label{lp:trigonometric-positivity}
 0\le-3\frac{\zeta'}\zeta(\sigma)
       -4\operatorname{Re}\frac{D'}D(\sigma+iv,\chi)
       -\operatorname{Re}\frac{D'}D(\sigma+2iv,\chi^2).$$ Indeed this follows term by term in the absolutely convergent prime-power expansions; primes dividing the modulus contribute only the positive zeta term. Also $-\zeta'/\zeta(\sigma)=1/(\sigma-1)+O(1)$ near $1$.

Suppose $\rho=\beta+iv$ were a zero in <a href="#lp:zero-free-region" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:zero-free-region">[lp:zero-free-region]</a>, and set $\sigma=1+20cT^2/L$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lp:local-log-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:local-log-derivative">29</a> at heights $v$ and $2v$. The evaluation points lie in the respective inner disks, and $\rho$ lies in the first zero sum, because $T^2/L=o(r_*)$. No zero has real part greater than $1$, by the absolutely convergent Euler product. All terms in the zero sums therefore contribute nonpositively to the negative real logarithmic derivatives. Retaining the term at $\rho$, and using $0<\sigma-\beta\le21cT^2/L$, bounds the right-hand side of <a href="#lp:trigonometric-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:trigonometric-positivity">[lp:trigonometric-positivity]</a> by $$\left(\frac{3}{20c}-\frac{4}{21c}+O_C(1)\right)\frac L{T^2}
 =\left(-\frac{17}{420c}+O_C(1)\right)\frac L{T^2}.$$ Here the errors are $O_C(T^2/r_*)=O_C(L/T^2)$. Choosing a sufficiently small fixed $c>0$ gives a contradiction.

For $s$ in the region of <a href="#lp:log-derivative-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:log-derivative-bound">[lp:log-derivative-bound]</a>, apply the local formula centered at $1+r_*+i\operatorname{Im}s$. Every zero in its sum has absolute imaginary part between $1$ and $x^3$, since $2\le|\operatorname{Im}s|\le x^3/2$ and $r_*=o(1)$. By <a href="#lp:zero-free-region" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:zero-free-region">[lp:zero-free-region]</a>, its horizontal distance from $s$ is at least $cT^2/(2L)$. The $O_C(T^2)$ zero terms and the local error therefore total $O_C(L)$, proving <a href="#lp:log-derivative-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:log-derivative-bound">[lp:log-derivative-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Mellin inversion and the prime polynomial

The zero-free strip permits a short contour shift while keeping the imaginary part away from zero. Smoothing the interval first makes the horizontal edges and discarded Mellin tails uniformly negligible.

<details id="proof-stmt-lp-zero-free-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lp-zero-free</summary>

<a id="lp:smoothing-error"></a>
*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lp:prime-polynomial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:prime-polynomial">23</a>.* We first establish the analogous bound with the von Mangoldt weight. Let $\delta=L^{-A-4}$. Smooth the indicator of $I/N\subset[1,2]$ by convolution with a nonnegative smooth kernel of width $\delta$. This gives $0\le g\le1$, supported in $[1/2,3]$, whose difference from the indicator is supported within $O(\delta)$ of its endpoints, and with $\|g^{(j)}\|_\infty\ll_j\delta^{-j}$. The construction also applies when $I$ is shorter than $\delta N$. Since $\Lambda(n)\le\log n$, the error in replacing the interval by $g(n/N)$ is $$\label{lp:smoothing-error}
 O\!\left((\delta N+1)\frac{\log(3N)}N\right)
       =O(L^{-A-2}),$$ uniformly in $I$.

<a id="lp:mellin-decay"></a>
<a id="lp:mellin-inversion"></a>
Define the Mellin transform by $$\widehat g(z)=\int_0^\infty g(w)w^z\,\frac{dw}{w}.$$ On every fixed bounded real-part strip, integration by parts gives $$\label{lp:mellin-decay}
 |\widehat g(u+iv)|\ll_j
       L^{(j+1)(A+5)}(1+|v|)^{-j}.$$ Also $|\widehat g(u+iv)|\ll1$ there, by absolute integration. Mellin inversion and the absolutely convergent logarithmic derivative on $\operatorname{Re}z=1/L$ yield $$\label{lp:mellin-inversion}
 \sum_n\Lambda(n)\chi(n)n^{-1+it}g(n/N)
 =\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{1/L-i\infty}^{1/L+i\infty}
       \widehat g(z)N^z
       \left(-\frac{D'}D(1-it+z,\chi)\right)dz.$$ On this full line, absolute convergence gives $$\left|\frac{D'}D(1+1/L+i u,\chi)\right|
 \le\sum_n\frac{\Lambda(n)}{n^{1+1/L}}
       =-\frac{\zeta'}\zeta(1+1/L)\ll L,$$ at every height $u$.

Choose a fixed $C_5>A+6$ and put $H_0=L^{C_5}$. Then choose a fixed integration-by-parts order $j$ large enough that <a href="#lp:mellin-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:mellin-decay">[lp:mellin-decay]</a> makes the two tails with $|\operatorname{Im}z|>H_0$ in <a href="#lp:mellin-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:mellin-inversion">[lp:mellin-inversion]</a> $O(L^{-A-2})$. Explicitly their bound is $$O_j\!\left(L^{1+(j+1)(A+5)}H_0^{1-j}\right),$$ since $N^{1/L}\ll1$. Fix $B_0>C_5+2$. For $L^{B_0}\le|t|\le x^2$, every point in the rectangle $$-\frac{cT^2}{2L}\le\operatorname{Re}z\le\frac1L,
       \qquad |\operatorname{Im}z|\le H_0$$ has <a id="lp:contour-heights"></a>$$\label{lp:contour-heights}
 2\le|-t+\operatorname{Im}z|
       \le x^2+H_0<\frac{x^3}{2}$$ for large $x$. Thus Lemma <a href="#lp:zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lp:zero-free">30</a> applies throughout the rectangle. There are no zeros or poles of the logarithmic derivative inside it; in particular, the possible principal-character pole at $z=it$ is outside it.

<a id="lp:mangoldt-estimate"></a>
Shift the truncated contour to $\operatorname{Re}z=-cT^2/(2L)$. The horizontal edges are $O(L^{-A-2})$, by <a href="#lp:log-derivative-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:log-derivative-bound">[lp:log-derivative-bound]</a> and <a href="#lp:mellin-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:mellin-decay">[lp:mellin-decay]</a>, increasing the already fixed order $j$ if necessary. On the new vertical segment, $$|N^z|=\exp\left(-\frac{cT^2\log N}{2L}\right)
       \ll\exp(-c\tau T^2/2).$$ Its remaining factors and length cost at most a fixed power of $L$. Since $\exp(-c\tau T^2/2)$ is smaller than every prescribed fixed power of $L^{-1}$, the shifted integral is $O(L^{-A-2})$. Together with <a href="#lp:smoothing-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:smoothing-error">[lp:smoothing-error]</a>, this proves, uniformly for all subintervals $I\subset[N,2N]$, $$\label{lp:mangoldt-estimate}
 \sum_{n\in I}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)n^{-1+it}\ll L^{-A-2}.$$

Prime powers of exponent at least two contribute in absolute value at most $O(N^{-1/2}(\log(3N))^2)$: there are $O(\sqrt N\log(3N))$ possible bases and exponents with $N\le p^a\le2N$, and every summand has size $O(\log(3N)/N)$. This is smaller than every fixed power of $L^{-1}$ in the present range. Removing them from <a href="#lp:mangoldt-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:mangoldt-estimate">[lp:mangoldt-estimate]</a> gives the same uniform bound for $\sum_{p\in I}(\log p)\chi(p)p^{-1+it}$. Finally, partial summation against $1/\log u$, whose value and total variation on $[N,2N]$ are $O_{\tau}(1/L)$, removes the logarithmic weight and proves <a href="#lp:prime-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lp:prime-estimate">[lp:prime-estimate]</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The proof has two analytic inputs. The one-sided estimate replaces prime slots in composite terms by rough slots. Successful marking brings the resulting centered statistic under the two-sided estimate. Averaging the fixed marks then gives interior moments; the probability argument recovers the full law. Appendix 9 supplies the long-prime and logarithmic-phase estimates used in the analytic branches.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-map",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [

          "exact_quote": "The proof has two analytic inputs. The one-sided estimate\nreplaces prime slots in composite terms by rough slots. Successful\nmarking brings the resulting centered statistic under the two-sided\nestimate. Averaging the fixed marks then gives ",

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  box/.style={draw=black!65,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,\n    text width=5.55cm,minimum height=1.03cm,inner sep=5pt,font=\\small},\n  arr/.style={-{Latex[length=2mm]},thick},\n  node distance=8mm and 7mm]\n\\node[box,fill=blue!5] (det)\n  {Determinant graph and signed memory\\\\\n   Sections~\\ref{sec:determinant}--\\ref{mem:section}};\n\\node[box,fill=blue!5,right=of det] (dil)\n  {Dilation-graph inputs and endpoint estimates\\\\\n   Section~\\ref{sec:correlations}};\n\\node[box,below=of det] (one)\n  {One-sided marked correlation\\\\Theorem~\\ref{thm:type-II}};\n\\node[box,below=of dil] (two)\n  {Two-sided marked correlation\\\\Theorem~\\ref{thm:two-sided}};\n\\node[box,below=11mm of $(one.south)!.5!(two.south)$,\n      text width=9cm] (interior)\n  {Presieve, replacement of prime slots, and averaging of marks\\\\\n   Interior factorial moments: Theorem~\\ref{thm:interior}};\n\\node[box,below=of interior,text width=9cm,fill=blue!5] (pd)\n  {Size-biased sampling, full mass, and decreasing rearrangement\\\\\n   Poisson--Dirichlet limit: Theorem~\\ref{thm:main}};\n\\draw[arr] (det) -- node[right,font=\\scriptsize,align=left]\n  {major-term estimate\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:major-arcs}} (one);\n\\draw[arr] (dil) -- (two);\n\\draw[arr] (one.south) -- (interior.north west);\n\\draw[arr] (two.south) -- (interior.north east);\n\\draw[arr] (interior) -- (pd);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram with five rectangular boxes arranged in four vertical levels:\n- Top level: Two boxes side-by-side:\n  - Left box: 'Determinant graph and signed memory Sections 3–4'\n  - Right box: 'Dilation-graph inputs and endpoint estimates Section 6'\n- Second level: Two boxes:\n  - Left box: 'One-sided marked correlation Theorem 3.1', connected from the top-left box by a downward arrow labeled to the right with 'major-term estimate Section 5'\n  - Right box: 'Two-sided marked correlation Theorem 6.1', connected from the top-right box by a downward arrow\n- Third level: One centered box:\n  - 'Presieve, replacement of prime slots, and averaging of marks Interior factorial moments: Theorem 7.1', with incoming downward diagonal arrows from both 'One-sided marked correlation Theorem 3.1' and 'Two-sided marked correlation Theorem 6.1'\n- Bottom level: One centered box:\n  - 'Size-biased sampling, full mass, and decreasing rearrangement Poisson–Dirichlet limit: Theorem 1.1', connected from the third-level box by a downward vertical arrow"

      "caption": "A schematic lifespan of a prime from group $i$. All active visits and both ghost endpoints have its common line; the pending visit at $4$ misses that line.       "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.64cm,y=1cm,\n  hit/.style={circle,draw=black,fill=white,inner sep=2.4pt},\n  active/.style={circle,draw=blue!65!black,fill=blue!25,inner sep=2.4pt},\n  ghost/.style={diamond,draw=black,fill=black!15,inner sep=2.4pt}]\n\\draw[thick,black!55] (0,0)--(7,0);\n\\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (1,0)--(2,0);\n\\foreach \\j in {0,...,7}\n  \\node[font=\\small,above] at (\\j,.23) {$\\j$};\n\\node[ghost] at (0,0) {};\n\\node[active] at (1,0) {};\n\\node[active] at (2,0) {};\n\\node[hit] at (3,0) {};\n\\node[circle,draw=black,dashed,fill=white,inner sep=2.4pt] at (4,0) {};\n\\node[active] at (5,0) {};\n\\node[hit] at (6,0) {};\n\\node[ghost] at (7,0) {};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=center,below] at (0,-.22) {ghost\\\\birth};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,below] at (1,-.22) {active};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,below] at (2,-.22) {active};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=center,below] at (3,-.22) {pending\\\\hit};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=center,below] at (4,-.22) {pending\\\\miss};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,below] at (5,-.22) {active};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=center,below] at (6,-.22) {pending\\\\hit};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=center,below] at (7,-.22) {ghost\\\\death};\n\\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,mirror,amplitude=4pt}]\n  (.93,-.88)--(2.07,-.88)\n  node[midway,below=5pt,font=\\scriptsize] {first active run};\n\\node[font=\\scriptsize,below] at (5,-.95) {second active run};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
